179 
Review of Monetary Policy from a Broad Perspective, Bank of Japan 
Experts' Commentaries 
Professor FUKUDA Shin-ichi, The University of Tokyo 
The implementation of unconventional monetary policy by the Bank of Japan was a challenge that 
ventured into uncharted territory, beyond the conventional monetary policy framework. Along the way, 
however, the Bank appears to have gradually left behind its conventional role, in a spiral where the more 
the Bank expanded its market interventions through unconventional monetary policy measures, the more 
the expectations for the Bank’s role grew. Below, I would like to discuss three issues that can be 
considered important in reviewing developments in economic activity and prices in Japan over the past 
25 years. 
The first point of discussion is whether stagnation in economic activity and prices during this period was 
cyclical or structural. If stagnation originates from cyclical demand shortages, monetary policy is an 
effective measure for stimulating economic activity and prices. This argument retains a certain degree of 
validity even after nominal interest rates have reached the effective lower bound. This idea seems to 
serve as the basis for the statement in the review of monetary policy from a broad perspective (hereinafter, 
the Review) that "large-scale monetary easing worked to push up economic activity and prices." If 
stagnation is due to structural supply-side issues, on the other hand, the effectiveness of monetary policy 
is significantly undermined. It seems that a large share of the structural issues during this period were 
beyond the scope of what monetary policy is meant to solve. Even if the Bank could play a role in this 
regard, its contribution was likely limited. 
Looking back over the past 25 years, Japan has experienced five economic troughs: in January 1999, 
January 2002, March 2009, November 2012, and May 2020. From a business-cycle perspective, it was 
natural for the Bank to strengthen its unconventional monetary policy measures in ways that roughly 
corresponded to these troughs. The Global Financial Crisis that began in fall 2008 and the onset of the 
pandemic in 2020, in particular, caused serious temporal damage to Japan's economy. It was significant 
that the Bank at the time decided to implement ultra-large-scale monetary easing that transcended the 
conventional monetary policy framework, aiming to restore market functioning, which had been seriously 
impaired. 
At the same time, Japan's economy experienced a pronounced downward trend in its potential growth 
rate over the past 25 years. According to the Bank's estimation, the potential growth rate, which exceeded 
4 percent in the early 1990s, fell below 1 percent in the second half of fiscal 1998 and has since followed 
a moderate downward trend, albeit with ups and downs. During this period, the Bank strengthened its 
unconventional monetary policy measures at various stages, but there are no signs that these initiatives 
led to improvement in the potential growth rate. Focusing in particular on the period after the introduction 
of large-scale monetary easing in 2013, the potential growth rate, which exceeded 1 percent at the time 
easing was introduced, continued on a declining trend and finally fell to around 0.2 percent by 2019. The 
Review avoids drawing a definitive conclusion regarding the medium- to long-term impact of monetary 
policy on the supply-side of the economy, suggesting only that it may have had both positive and negative 
sides. Given the downward trend in the potential growth rate, however, it is hard to imagine that there 


 
          Review of Monetary Policy from a Broad Perspective, Bank of Japan 
Experts' Commentaries 
180 
have been positive effects. 
In this regard, the joint statement of the government and the Bank (the Accord) issued when the Bank 
set the price stability target of 2 percent in January 2013 offers an important perspective. The statement 
suggests that, while the Bank aims to achieve the price stability target through monetary easing, the 
government needs to strengthen the competitiveness and growth potential of Japan's economy. That is, 
this statement implies that monetary policy can fulfill its intended function if the government takes 
initiatives to strengthen the competitiveness and growth potential, such as structural reform of the 
economy. In reality, however, amid insufficient progress in the government's initiatives, the Bank's 
unprecedented monetary easing alone was further strengthened. The potential growth rate moved on a 
downward trend as a result. 
The second point of discussion is whether prolonged low inflation expectations were the cause or the 
result of stagnation in economic activity and prices over the past 25 years. If low inflation expectations 
are the main cause of such stagnation, it would be effective for the Bank to strongly commit to the price 
stability target of 2 percent and push ahead with large-scale monetary easing. From the perspective that 
Japan's economy fell into a "bad" deflationary equilibrium among multiple equilibria, in particular, raising 
inflation expectations alone could be enough to move the economy toward a "good" equilibrium. On the 
other hand, if low inflation expectations are the result of stagnation in economic activity and prices, raising 
people's inflation expectations would prove difficult without resolving the root causes of the stagnation. 
Especially when the causes of stagnation lie in structural issues rather than cyclical demand shortages, 
it would be hard for the Bank to raise people's inflation expectations, no matter how bold its monetary 
policy might be. 
The aforementioned notion – that raising people's inflation expectations is an effective measure in 
restoring stagnant economic activity and prices in Japan – was first argued by Professor Paul Krugman 
(currently at the City University of New York). Many researchers, both in Japan and abroad, subsequently 
advanced detailed arguments on the importance of monetary policy in influencing expectations. The 
Bank was also the first central bank in the world to adopt forward guidance, referred to at the time as the 
"policy duration effect," and worked to reinforce the effectiveness of its policy even as nominal interest 
rates faced the effective lower bound. 
The Bank's forward guidance generated expectations that the ultra-low interest rate policy would 
continue for the time being, leading to its success in lowering long-term nominal interest rates. The 
Bank's large-scale Japanese government bond (JGB) purchases including longer-term JGBs, in 
particular, heightened the effect of forward guidance, pushing nominal interest rates down across the 
entire yield curve. However, forward guidance was unable to raise inflation expectations in a sustainable 
manner. The Review argues that "large-scale monetary easing had an impact to some degree on inflation 
expectations." It did, temporarily. However, the impact did not last and inflation expectations remained at 
a low level even as the Bank strengthened its monetary easing. 
Looking back over the past 25 years, Japan experienced significant demand shortages during periods 


 
181 
Review of Monetary Policy from a Broad Perspective, Bank of Japan 
Experts' Commentaries 
such as 1998 to 1999, 2001 to 2003, and 2009 to 2011. These shortages partially led to chronic deflation 
and low inflation expectations. Meanwhile, as the Bank strengthened monetary easing, the output gap 
turned positive in 2017 and indicated significant excess demand during the period from 2018 to 2019. 
Nevertheless, even amid major improvement in the output gap, the situation in which wages and prices 
did not increase continued. 
The Review states that an increase in the labor supply from women and seniors was one of the factors 
contributing to the situation where wages did not increase. Although this argument is of interest, there is 
another possibility that the stagnation in wages and prices was caused by other structural factors in the 
labor market, such as job mismatch. The Review also points to the global disinflationary trend and the 
declining trend in Japan's international competitiveness as other causes of stagnation. In addition to 
these factors, however, other structural problems in Japan may have been causes of stagnation. In 
particular, corporate business dynamics in Japan did not make sufficient progress during this period and, 
in turn, many unprofitable firms remained in the market. This is also a potential reason for the situation 
where wages and prices did not increase. 
However, as inflation has materialized worldwide, the situation of prices in Japan is also changing. Since 
April 2022, in particular, the year-on-year rate of change in the consumer price index (CPI, all items less 
fresh food) has constantly exceeded 2 percent. The initial increase in prices was mainly triggered by 
external shocks – import price hikes. Due in part to the impact of wage hikes, however, inflation has 
gradually spilled over to service prices in Japan. On the other hand, nominal wage inflation has trailed 
behind price inflation, even though labor shortages have grown more acute. Under these circumstances, 
the mindset of households has not clearly improved. What is more problematic is that many of the 
domestic structural issues causing the situation where prices did not increase have not yet been fully 
resolved. It may take some time to determine whether Japan can truly achieve the price stability target 
of 2 percent in a sustainable manner. 
The third point of discussion concerns the side effects of large-scale easing on capital flows in Japan's 
financial markets. The Review points out that the functioning of the JGB market deteriorated due to large-
scale easing, particularly after the introduction of Yield Curve Control. On the other hand, the Review 
also states that there is no evidence that large-scale easing has impeded financial intermediation 
activities. In reality, however, capital flows within the macroeconomy have shifted significantly under the 
ultra-large-scale monetary easing measures implemented over the past 25 years. 
In Japan's economy until 25 years ago, the majority of household savings flowed to the corporate sector 
through the intermediation of the financial sector. This is in contrast to the situation over the past 25 
years. Even though ultra-low interest rates compressed financial institutions' interest margins on loans 
and other assets as well as lowered credit risks, recovery in corporate demand for funds remained limited 
and the majority of household savings were redirected to the government sector. This likely structured a 
relationship in which the government's ever-expanding deficit is ultimately financed by the vast financial 
assets of households. That is, the allocation of households' abundant savings, intermediated by the 


 
          Review of Monetary Policy from a Broad Perspective, Bank of Japan 
Experts' Commentaries 
182 
financial sector, shifted from the private corporate sector – which has growth potential – to the 
government sector – which has lower productivity. This may have led to the decline in Japan's growth 
potential. 
The fundamental cause of these shifts in capital flows lies with the government sector, which has been 
unable to put the brakes on the expansion of its debt. It seems large-scale monetary easing has 
unintentionally made it easier for the government to finance its debt, leading to a loosening of fiscal 
discipline. The Review notes that the objective of the Bank's monetary policy is price stability and not 
"monetary finance." However, given the anticipated accumulation of government debt going forward, an 
important challenge for the Bank will be how to respond to the JGB markets as it normalizes monetary 
policy. 


 
183 
Review of Monetary Policy from a Broad Perspective, Bank of Japan 
Experts' Commentaries 
Professor HOSHI Takeo, The University of Tokyo 
The objective of the comprehensive review (hereinafter, the Review) was to examine the series of 
monetary easing policies implemented by the Bank since the late 1990s, while keeping in mind the 
"interrelationships with various fields of the economy and finance," and to derive "useful insights for future 
policy management." The completed Review fulfills this objective. 
The Review first identifies three causes of the prolonged deflation, which underpinned monetary easing: 
(1) chronic lack of demand, (2) intensified price competition due to globalization and the spread of 
technological innovations, and (3) the entrenched practices based on the assumption that wages and 
prices are unlikely to rise. It then analyzes, using the latest data and methods, the unconventional 
monetary policies implemented during two periods – before and after the early 2010s – and examines 
their effects and side effects. By the early 2010s, the Bank had pioneered policies that would later be 
termed unconventional monetary policies, such as zero interest rate policy, forward guidance, and 
quantitative easing. The 2010 "Comprehensive Monetary Easing Policy" expanded the purchase of 
assets other than government bonds by creating funds for asset purchases. Although the series of 
unconventional monetary policies were not enough to eliminate deflationary conditions entirely, the 
Review points out that the policies were effective in stabilizing the financial system by supplying ample 
liquidity to financial markets in the aftermath of Japan's banking crisis in the late 1990s and again in the 
midst of the global financial crisis in the late 2000s. 
In January 2013, a 2% price stability target was set, and Quantitative and Qualitative Monetary Easing 
(QQE) was introduced in April. Chronic demand shortages were finally alleviated, and deflation, in the 
sense of sustained price declines, ended. However, inflation rates remained low. Subsequently, QQE 
was expanded with additional measures, such as negative interest rate policy and yield curve control. 
The practices based on the assumption that wages and prices would not rise did not change significantly 
even with these extraordinarily expansionary measures. The Review recognizes that QQE since 2013 
had some side effects, such as reduced functionality in the government bond market. Nevertheless, the 
Review concludes that, the positive effects outweigh the negative ones, at least for now. 
The Review highlights three major implications for future monetary policy management. First, although 
the effects of unconventional monetary policies are more uncertain and can have side effects compared 
to conventional policy that targets short-term interest rates, the unconventional policies conducted by the 
Bank, including QQE since 2013, remain valuable tools for future use. At the same time, it stresses the 
need for caution regarding measures that expand central bank balance sheets from the perspective of 
financial soundness. Second, the 2% price stability target should be sustained, partly to avoid over-
reliance on unconventional monetary policies. Lastly, while appreciating the benefits of coordination 
between fiscal and monetary policies, the Review concludes that monetary financing of fiscal deficits, 
which could undermine confidence in the currency, must be avoided. 
I have no major disagreements with the conclusions of the Review. It provides a comprehensive 
summary of 25 years of monetary policy and examines its effects and side effects. Dividing the period 


 
          Review of Monetary Policy from a Broad Perspective, Bank of Japan 
Experts' Commentaries 
184 
into the pre-2010s and thereafter is appropriate, as the scale of the monetary easing since 2013 differ 
substantially from earlier unconventional monetary policies, which could have had larger effects and side 
effects. The implications for future policy derived from the analysis are also reasonable. Of course, not 
all issues are resolved by this Review. As it acknowledges, many of the side effects of unconventional 
monetary policies may emerge over time. Let me highlight four issues that require continued attention 
going forward. 
First, while the Review correctly argues that the long-term impacts of the monetary easing on resource 
allocation is not yet clear, a more detailed examination will be necessary soon. The most recent 
significant monetary easing was in response to the COVID-19 crisis, and the potential distortion of 
resource allocation was not a primary concern during such emergency. But now, as the economy 
recovers from the crisis, it is essential to find out the extent of such distortion and come up with a timeline 
for the resolution. From a monetary policy perspective, it is also critical to discern how much of the 
resource allocation distortions stemmed from monetary easing and how much from other policies, such 
as fiscal support for businesses and industries. 
Second, regarding the impact of large-scale monetary easing on the functionality of the government bond 
market, further analysis is necessary to evaluate how much of the observed decline in the functions can 
be attributed to monetary easing. Market functionality could deteriorate independently of monetary policy. 
The need for unconventional monetary policies typically arises under circumstances where market 
functions are already impaired by factors such as the zero lower bound on interest rates. Careful analysis 
of how much market functionality declined due to monetary easing is crucial, along with exploring more 
objective measures of market functionality. 
Third, whether large-scale monetary easing led to a decline of fiscal discipline demands further research, 
including political-economic analysis. Fiscal discipline is critical for maintaining confidence in the 
currency and price stability, as the Review points out. While central banks cannot directly enforce fiscal 
discipline, their monetary policies may influence it indirectly. Should monetary policies be designed to 
encourage fiscal discipline through government bond markets? Furthering explicit cooperation between 
governments and central banks to ensure fiscal discipline and price stability may be necessary. Although 
this issue extends beyond the Review's scope, further study that involve researchers outside the Bank 
would be useful. 
Lastly, as unconventional monetary policies involving balance sheet expansion entail financial risks for 
central banks, the financial risks faced by the Bank, which already has significantly expanded balance 
sheet, must be considered. With monetary policy normalization and rising interest rates, these risks are 
already here. The Bank should carefully start a dialogue with the market to assess the degree of the risk 
and how to maintain the Bank's operational capabilities. 
 


 
 
185 
Review of Monetary Policy from a Broad Perspective, Bank of Japan 
Experts' Commentaries 
Professor ITO Takatoshi, Columbia University 
The Bank of Japan conducted a comprehensive review of its monetary and financial stability policies 
over the past 25 years (hereinafter, the Review). This initiative holds substantial significance from both 
academic and policy perspectives. The Review consists of three sections. Section 1 discusses 
developments in the real economy since 1990, covering topics such as economic growth, employment, 
and wages. Section 2 considers monetary policy since 1998. Section 3 presents the implications of the 
Review's findings for the future conduct of monetary policy. Due to space limitations, this commentary 
focuses primarily on Section 2. As a whole, the Review appears to provide well-structured analyses of 
how monetary policy has influenced economic activity and prices. Moreover, many of the analytical 
results presented in the Review align with the existing literature. Therefore, the Review can be evaluated 
as a well-executed empirical analysis of the effects of monetary policy and its transmission mechanism. 
However, the Review does not raise or analyze issues regarding whether or not monetary policy 
decisions at each point in time were appropriate. This may be inevitable, considering that the Review 
was conducted by the Bank itself. In addition, it should be noted that the Review lacks sufficient analysis 
from the perspective of international comparisons. In fact, some of the phenomena examined in the 
Review were unique to Japan, while others were common worldwide. Furthermore, some occurred (or 
were carried out) first in Japan, while others occurred or were first carried out in other economies. 
However, the Review only makes such distinctions for a few issues. Also, it provides limited discussion 
and consideration of the differences between Japan and other economies in terms of central banks and 
their monetary policies. 
Section 2 on monetary policy since 1998 offers a comprehensive assessment, referring to many 
complementary studies. The takeaways of this section are as follows. The first point concerns the effects 
of monetary policy measures implemented during the period from 1999 to 2012. During this period, the 
Bank introduced the Zero Interest Rate Policy (ZIRP, 1999-2000), the Quantitative Easing Policy (QE, 
2001-2006), and Forward Guidance (which it called the "policy duration effect" at the time). However, 
these policies did not push down real interest rates significantly, and thus did not sufficiently stimulate 
economic activity and prices. The second point concerns the effects of monetary policy introduced after 
2013. During this period, the Bank adopted a price stability target of 2 percent in January 2013 and 
introduced Quantitative and Qualitative Monetary Easing (QQE) in April of the same year. Furthermore, 
the Bank introduced the Negative Interest Rate Policy (NIRP) in January 2016 and Yield Curve Control 
(YCC) in September of the same year. With these policies, the Bank assumed the following two 
transmission mechanisms: (1) the Bank would raise inflation expectation rates to 2 percent by influencing 
expectations, and (2) it would reduce nominal interest rates by means of QQE. The first mechanism did 
not subsequently emerge. However, the Bank did succeed with the second mechanism by means of 
NIRP and YCC. The third point concerns the side effects of monetary policy. The Review gives the 
following results of examining the impact of QQE on the functioning of financial markets, the functioning 
of financial intermediation, and growth potential: (1) the prolonged implementation of QQE lowered the 
functioning of the Japanese government bond (JGB) market; (2) QQE did not impede the functioning of 


 
 
          Review of Monetary Policy from a Broad Perspective, Bank of Japan 
Experts' Commentaries 
186 
financial intermediation; and (3) looking at the impact on the supply side of the economy, QQE promoted 
the accumulation of capital as well as human capital, by mitigating unemployment, while having a 
contractive impact, including a decline in productivity, by distorting resource allocation. 
In the remainder of this commentary, I would like to express my views on these conclusions of the Review. 
As illustrated by Ito and Hoshi (2020), monetary policy since 1999 can be examined by dividing the entire 
period into four sub-periods. Here, I present my thoughts in terms of this framework. 
The first period is from 1999 to 2008. It seems to me that the Review could have offered a more in-depth 
analysis of the reasons why ZIRP and QE did not fully achieve their intended effects during this period. 
If an analysis of this issue is to be conducted, the following hypotheses might be considered useful. 
Hypothesis A is that QE – which mainly consisted of ZIRP and purchases of shorter-term Japanese 
government securities – and forward guidance were by nature not sufficient measures to stimulate the 
economy. Hypothesis B is that ZIRP and QE themselves were effective but were terminated prematurely. 
Furthermore, the following hypotheses B1 to B3 can be considered as the background of such premature 
termination. Hypothesis B1 is that the Bank regarded zero (or near-zero) inflation as the state of price 
stability at the time. Hypothesis B2 is that the Bank was skeptical about the effects of these measures 
and that this unconfident attitude led to its failure of communication with the public. Hypothesis B3 is that, 
while the Bank aimed for positive inflation at the time, it decided to terminate these policy measures 
prematurely due to an overly optimistic outlook for economic activity and inflation. Hypothesis C is that 
the termination of QE in 2006 was not premature. Under this hypothesis, Japan's economy continued to 
grow even after QE ended while the inflation rate remained positive, and then approached full 
employment just before the Global Financial Crisis (GFC) in 2008. If the GFC had not occurred, Japan's 
economy would have overcome deflation and continued on its growth path. However, the Review does 
not present this kind of hypothesis-testing analysis. 
The second period is from the onset of the GFC in 2008 to 2012. During this period, the Japanese yen 
appreciated, the economy remained stagnant, and deflation worsened. It is widely believed that these 
developments occurred because the Bank of Japan's QE remained extremely small compared to the 
larger-scale QE implemented by central banks in the United States and Europe. The Review highlights 
the declining trend in the natural rate of interest as the background of deflation and points out several 
possible reasons for this trend (p.12 and Appendix 2). The reasons presented in the Review seem 
plausible. However, considering that the declining trend in government bond yields since 1990 was 
observed across advanced economies, it also seems important to explore potential factors that could 
apply to other economies as well. For example, why did capital efficiency – i.e. the marginal productivity 
of capital, which should be consistent with real interest rates in the long run – continue its declining trend? 
This question seems important, especially considering that technological progress – which promotes 
productivity growth – had been picking up speed during this period (e.g., progress in information and 
communication technology, ICT, and artificial intelligence technology, AI; advances in initiatives such as 
digital transformation, DX). Was Japan the only country that failed in the adoption of ICT, AI, and DX? 
Moreover, the impact of a declining birthrate and an aging population on the natural rate of interest 


 
 
187 
Review of Monetary Policy from a Broad Perspective, Bank of Japan 
Experts' Commentaries 
(Charts 1-1-9 and A2-4) is a highly intriguing mechanism, and this is likely a contributing factor to the 
decline in the natural rate of interest in Japan. However, such demographic trends are not common to 
other countries, and this mechanism therefore does not provide a sufficient explanation for the global 
declining trend in the natural rate of interest. In addition, the figure of −2 percent indicated in Chart A2-4 
as the impact of demographic trends on the natural rate of interest seems excessively large. 
The Review also highlights a "behavior and mindset based on the assumption that wages and prices will 
not increase easily" as another reason for deflation, and examines this issue from various perspectives. 
This reasoning seems to be the most plausible explanation for deflation. This mechanism makes it 
possible to explain Japan's uniqueness compared with other economies. All of the charts related to this 
topic are important and of interest. Appendix 4 and Appendix 5 also add to the persuasiveness of this 
argument. 
The third period is the three years from 2013 to 2015, following the introduction of QQE. During this 
period, the Bank adopted the price stability target of 2 percent in January 2013, started QQE in April of 
the same year, and expanded QQE in October 2014. It is widely understood that these policy measures 
stimulated the economy and boosted inflation rates, an understanding that is met with few opposing 
views. The Review also views monetary policies implemented during this period positively, although there 
are various views regarding the transmission channels through which QQE stimulated the economy. The 
Review states that the Bank assumed "raising inflation expectations to 2 percent by influencing 
expectations" to be one of the main transmission channels of QQE but that this channel did not function 
as intended. Specifically, it notes that inflation expectations rose from 2013 through 2014 but 
subsequently declined through 2015 "due to factors such as the fall in crude oil prices" (p.40). However, 
some economists express another view, that the stagnation in consumption following the consumption 
tax hike in 2014 caused the decline in inflation expectations. That is, this view argues, even though the 
aim of achieving the price stability target of 2 percent with a time horizon of two years – which was 
announced at the time QQE was introduced – was almost within reach, the consumption tax hike foiled 
the achievement of the target. The Review notes that even in academia, "views are divided regarding 
the extent to which monetary easing can influence expectations when nominal short-term interest rates 
are at the effective lower bound" (p.40 and Appendix 7), but further analysis to assess the background 
of the initial rise in inflation expectations from 2013 to 2014 seems called for. 
The Review also argues that the impact through transmission channels other than expectations – such 
as increases in investment and consumption resulting from the depreciation of the yen and rising stock 
prices – was significant. It further states that large-scale monetary easing pushed down nominal interest 
rates, mainly due to the stock effects of the Bank's JGB holdings, and that the extent to which the Bank's 
JGB purchases pushed down long-term interest rates was about one percentage point. It also notes that 
NIRP, introduced in January 2016, significantly pushed down not only short-term but also long-term 
interest rates. However, further analysis of the impact on long-term interest rates seems necessary. I 
think the significant decline in long-term interest rates can rather be considered as evidence that the 
channel of influencing inflation expectations through large-scale monetary easing did not function at all. 


 
 
          Review of Monetary Policy from a Broad Perspective, Bank of Japan 
Experts' Commentaries 
188 
The Review's analysis using the Bank's macroeconomic model (Quarterly Japanese Economic Model: 
Q-JEM) reveals that, compared to the scenario without QQE, "the size of the policy effects on the level 
of real GDP was estimated to be +1.3 to +1.8 percent, and on the year-on-year rate of change in 
consumer prices, +0.5 to +0.7 percentage points, respectively." Furthermore, the analysis using a large-
scale time series (Factor-Augmented Vector Autoregressive: FAVAR) model reveals that the decline in 
shadow rates resulting from large-scale monetary easing pushed up the year-on-year rate of change in 
consumer prices by around +0.6 to +1.1 percentage points. The results of these quantitative analyses 
seem plausible. 
I would also like to make a few comments on the impact of QQE on the functioning of financial markets, 
the functioning of financial intermediation, and the growth potential of the economy. There is a criticism 
that the prolonged expansion of QQE led to the loosening of the government's commitment to restraining 
the fiscal deficit (the "moral hazard" problem). However, I think that the moral hazard criticism is not 
applicable. In fact, the government commits to maintaining fiscal discipline in the "joint statement" on the 
inflation target published in January 2013. The "agreement" clarifies the policy responsibilities between 
the Bank and the government as follows: the Bank is responsible for achieving the inflation target, on the 
one hand, and the government is responsible for maintaining fiscal consolidation, on the other hand. 
The Review examined the impact of large-scale monetary easing on the functioning of financial markets 
by measuring liquidity indicators (transaction volume and the bid-ask spread) and relative price indicators 
(yield curve distortion). It presented the following three takeaways from the examination: (1) the Bank's 
purchase of JGBs – which is a flow variable – pushes up transaction volume, whereas an increase in the 
Bank's share of JGB holdings pushes volume down; (2) the Bank's purchase of JGBs compresses the 
bid-ask spread, while an increase in the Bank's share of JGB holdings expands the bid-ask spread 
discontinuously; and (3) an increase in the Bank's share of certain issues of JGB holdings and its conduct 
of continuous fixed-rate purchase operations lead to distortion in the yield curve. Although these findings 
seem plausible, the Review still does not provide sufficient evidence for the extent of the inefficiencies 
and the negative impact of large-scale monetary easing on capital markets and on the sovereign bond 
investment business. 
Meanwhile, there is a view that a central bank's balance sheet will not (or should not) go down to the 
level it stood at before the introduction of unconventional monetary policy. In other words, applying 
interest rates to excess reserves and maintaining an expanded balance sheet might be reasonable from 
the perspective of financial stability. On the other hand, another view holds that balance sheet policy 
might be an additional policy tool even in normal times. 
The fourth period is defined as the period from 2016 to the present. During this period, the Bank 
introduced NIRP in January 2016 and YCC in September of the same year. However, policies introduced 
during this period have received several strong criticisms. The first criticism is that the introduction of 
NIRP excessively flattened the yield curve, which led to compression of the profits of financial institutions 
holding JGBs and, in turn, deteriorating financial stability. YCC can be considered a policy for maintaining 


 
 
189 
Review of Monetary Policy from a Broad Perspective, Bank of Japan 
Experts' Commentaries 
the yield curve at a lower level but still with a positive slope. The Review offers the assessment that NIRP 
and YCC pushed down nominal interest rates but also concludes that the impact of flattening the yield 
curve was not so significant. The second criticism is that the side effects of QQE have become larger 
and the yen has depreciated as inflation increased beyond 2 percent (albeit more mildly compared to the 
United States and Europe). This perspective suggests that the Bank may have lagged behind in the 
process of normalizing monetary policy. The third criticism is that the prolonged JGB purchases through 
QQE induced the loosening of the government's commitment to restraining the fiscal deficit (the "moral 
hazard" problem). The Review argues that the Bank has continuously and consistently explained that its 
monetary policy is not aiming to support the monetary financing of government debt. Overall, it seems 
that the Review could have provided a more in-depth evaluation of the effects of the policies introduced 
during this period. 
In terms of risks faced by financial institutions, two factors can be considered important: a concentration 
of loans to specific sectors, such as real estate, and an increase in interest rate risk due to longer loan 
terms and maturities of bonds held by financial institutions. However, it also seems worth considering, 
as a principle of monetary policy, that the response to these risks should be to encourage banks to 
strengthen their capital through the use of policy measures, including the Counter-Cyclical Buffer 
regulation, and that central banks should not pay excessive attention to the functioning of financial 
intermediation when policy interest rates need to be changed by means of monetary policy conduct. 
The Review also argues, in the context of changes in the 2020s and the current situation of the economy, 
that the room for additional labor supply, including from women and seniors, is shrinking – which is 
consistent with the standard view in academia. This seems true from a quantitative perspective but, from 
a qualitative perspective, room for improvement remains. That is, additional qualitative improvement can 
be achieved by increasing productivity through measures such as job transitions to more advanced 
occupations, wage increases, and the extension of the retirement age for skilled workers. There also 
remains the issue of how long it will take time for the shift of firms' wage- and price-setting behavior to 
take root. Such behavior may shift completely if firms continue generating high profits and increasing 
base pay above the inflation rate in spring labor-management wage negotiations consecutively for the 
next year or two. If such a situation comes about, achievement of the inflation target of 2 percent may 
finally take root. 
Finally, in Section 3 on the implications for the future conduct of monetary policy, the Review states that 
the quantitative degree of the effects of unconventional monetary policies is uncertain. It further notes 
that unconventional monetary policy measures can bring about side effects and therefore the benefits 
and costs of such measures should be considered when the Bank considers adopting them in the future. 
It should be noted, however, that the Review does not propose specific ideas for how to quantify the 
costs and benefits of the policy measures. Due to space limitations, I will omit a discussion of individual 
policy measures. 
 


 
 
          Review of Monetary Policy from a Broad Perspective, Bank of Japan 
Experts' Commentaries 
190 
Reference 
Ito, Takatoshi, and Takeo Hoshi (2020), The Japanese Economy, Second Edition, The MIT Press. 


 
 
191 
Review of Monetary Policy from a Broad Perspective, Bank of Japan 
Experts' Commentaries 
Professor MURATA Keiko, Rissho University 
Since the introduction of Quantitative and Qualitative Monetary Easing (QQE) in 2013, the Bank has 
undertaken policy assessments at major turning points in its monetary policy conduct, such as the 
Comprehensive Assessment in September 2016 and the Assessment for Further Effective and 
Sustainable Monetary Easing in March 2021. The latest review of monetary policy from a broad 
perspective (hereinafter, the Review) looks back over the 25 years since the adoption of the Zero Interest 
Rate Policy (ZIRP) and then clarifies the factors behind Japan's prolonged deflationary trend. 
Furthermore, the Review assesses the effects and limitations of monetary policies during this period and 
then presents implications for monetary policy conduct going forward. It is significant that the Bank has 
reviewed its monetary policy conduct since the introduction of the ZIRP at this juncture, when it has 
shifted its policy stance toward tightening. 
One notable feature throughout the Review is that it conducts its examination from multiple viewpoints 
based on various models and datasets, without adhering to a specific theory or model. This approach is 
fitting with the name of a Review from a Broad Perspective. Fundamentally, no perfect economic model 
exists. In particular, the literature on the topic of the effective lower bound on nominal interest rates – a 
topic which extends beyond the standard macroeconomic model framework – remains underdeveloped. 
In this regard, the Bank's approach of not relying on a specific theory or model appears appropriate, 
taking into account the possibility that a variety of views exist in this area. In the Review, the Bank also 
makes an effort to supplement the discussion in a qualitative manner, using results derived from surveys 
of firms and households the Bank conducted itself. It furthermore had multiple opportunities for 
discussions with researchers from Japan and abroad and also conducted a Public Consultation to gather 
opinions from the general public. 
At the same time, in addition to the aforementioned efforts, the Review ensures readability by carefully 
selecting language and charts. This appears to reflect Governor Ueda's statement in his first speech as 
the Governor of the Bank, when he said he would endeavor to "make logical decisions and provide 
explanations as clearly as possible."  
The key lesson reaffirmed through the Review might be that deflation should not be allowed to persist. 
In the following section, I would like to discuss three issues from the perspective of the outlook for Japan's 
economy and finally offer some caveats and insights. 
1. Why Did Consumer Prices Start Rising in 2022? 
The Review carefully analyzes Japan's economy from the late 1990s following the burst of the bubble 
economy, breaking it down into three periods. It points out three factors as the background to prolonged 
moderate deflation through the early 2010s: (1) a decline in the natural rate of interest and a chronic 
shortage of demand; (2) downward pressure on prices from the supply side, including from globalization; 
and (3) entrenchment of the behavior and mindset based on the assumption that wages and prices will 
not increase easily. The Review then argues that the third factor in particular – which in some respects 


 
 
          Review of Monetary Policy from a Broad Perspective, Bank of Japan 
Experts' Commentaries 
192 
also resulted from prolonged moderate deflation – made it difficult for Japan's economy to overcome 
deflation. In fact, after the introduction of QQE, inflation shifted to a slightly positive trend, but the price 
stability target of 2 percent was not achieved. As the background to the underachievement of the target, 
the Review points to the additional labor supply from women and seniors as well as the entrenchment of 
the behavior and mindset based on the assumption that wages and prices will not increase easily. 
Import prices rose sharply in the 2020s. In evaluating this period, the Review states that there are 
indications of a change in the behavior and mindset based on the assumption that wages and prices will 
not increase easily, amid pronounced labor shortages and a substantial rise in import prices. The Review 
argues that, under these circumstances, medium- to long-term inflation expectations are also rising 
toward 2 percent. 
Taking into account the Review's analysis, the issue of whether people's long-term inflation expectations 
will stabilize at 2 percent in the future will likely depend on changes in firms' wage- and price-setting 
behavior, as well as the underlying factor – the behavior and mindset based on the assumption that 
wages and prices will not increase easily. While the Review points to signs of change, uncertainties 
remain in terms of determining whether a structural change has actually occurred. From the perspective 
of Evidence-Based Policy-Making, efforts are needed to verify whether such structural change has taken 
root going forward. 
The trigger for overcoming the deflationary trend in Japan was, in the end, import price inflation. The 
sharp rise in import prices beginning in the spring of 2021 marked the largest such increase since the 
second oil crisis in 1980, and continued for two years. This resulted in higher costs for firms, which were 
subsequently passed on to domestic prices. Prices of electricity, gas, and goods in particular also 
increased. As a result, consumer price inflation exceeded two percent, albeit with a lag of about a year 
relative to these developments. As this was taking place, both the government and the Bank advocated 
strongly for the necessity of wage hikes, and firms in fact raised wages to ensure adequate staffing and 
to maintain the living standards of their employees. Subsequently, service price inflation shifted to an 
increasing trend. In this context, inflation currently remains above 2 percent. 
2. How Should the Impact of Unconventional Monetary Policy on Inflation Be Assessed? 
The Comprehensive Assessment in 2016 stressed the importance of influencing expectations – inflation 
expectations formation – as a transmission mechanism of monetary easing under QQE. Specifically, it 
argued that the adaptive nature of expectations formation is a factor preventing inflation expectations 
from rising. Against this backdrop, the Bank introduced an inflation-overshooting commitment as a step 
to further strengthen the forward-looking expectations formation mechanism at that time. The 
Assessment for Further Effective and Sustainable Monetary Easing in 2021 maintained this 
understanding and noted two additional points: that the labor participation of women and seniors and 
productivity growth among firms had absorbed cost pressures; and that the entrenchment of the behavior 
and mindset based on the assumption that prices will not increase easily had served as an obstacle to 
achieving the target. 


 
 
193 
Review of Monetary Policy from a Broad Perspective, Bank of Japan 
Experts' Commentaries 
On the other hand, the latest Review concludes, in discussing the large-scale monetary easing since 
2013, that "influencing expectations alone was not sufficiently effective to anchor inflation at 2 percent." 
As factors behind this insufficient effectiveness, the Review stresses the behavior and mindset based on 
the assumption that wages and prices will not increase easily mentioned in the previous section, as well 
as the adaptive nature of expectations formation that the Bank had previously claimed. In other words, 
large-scale monetary easing appears to have achieved its goal eleven years after its introduction, with 
the support of import price inflation and labor shortages. 
The Review notes that the quantitative degree of the effects of unconventional monetary policy measures 
is uncertain compared with conventional measures, which are conducted by guiding short-term interest 
rates. Against this backdrop, the Review suggests that unconventional monetary policy measures cannot 
fully substitute for conventional measures. The Review then states that, if it becomes necessary going 
forward to implement unconventional monetary policy measures, it will be important to weigh the benefits 
and costs of such measures while taking into account developments in economic activity and prices as 
well as financial conditions at that point in time. 
The Review notes that Japan experienced the limitations of large-scale monetary easing and that these 
limitations were partially due to the entrenchment of the behavior and mindset based on the assumption 
that wages and prices will not increase easily – which monetary policy could not change. Taking this 
analysis into account, if Japan faces deflation again, it will be crucial for the government and the Bank 
to reach a common understanding and work together to prevent Japan's economy from falling into a 
situation where prolonged deflation leads to the entrenchment of the above-mentioned behavior and 
mindset. Also, in terms of weighing the benefits and costs of unconventional measures, it will be 
necessary to further consider the issues of what kind of costs should be assessed and over what time 
frame, as well as what methods should be used to properly quantify them. 
3. Side Effects of Prolonged Unconventional Monetary Policy Measures 
Although keeping short- and long-term interest rates at a low level helped support the economy, it also 
had side effects. The Review presents analyses of the impact on financial markets and the profits of 
financial institutions, including a decline in the functioning of the Japanese government bond (JGB) 
market and a contraction of financial institutions' lending margins. It also mentions the impact on the 
growth potential of the supply side of Japan's economy, as well as issues regarding fiscal discipline. 
Regarding fiscal discipline, in a low interest rate environment, income has continued shifting from the 
household sector – a creditor (an excess-saving sector) – to the government sector – a debtor. At the 
same time, due to lower interest payments, loose fiscal discipline has become a matter of course. It 
should be appreciated that the Review mentions this loosening of fiscal discipline, referring to the fact 
that this issue was pointed out in the exchange of views with the public. 
4. Other Points 
Finally, I would like to suggest two points as caveats and insights going forward. The first point concerns 


 
 
          Review of Monetary Policy from a Broad Perspective, Bank of Japan 
Experts' Commentaries 
194 
the issue I mentioned earlier of whether firms' wage- and price-setting behavior has actually changed. 
Given that the emergence of structural change currently remains unclear, according to the Review's 
quantitative analysis, it would be appropriate to continue monitoring the situation carefully for some time, 
including developments among small- and medium-sized firms. Also, changes in wages tend to lag 
behind changes in prices. Taking into account this characteristic of wages among other factors, it is 
desirable to define the price stability target as a medium- to long-term objective, similar to other advanced 
economies, and to conduct monetary policy with a certain degree of flexibility depending on the situation. 
The second point – this may be regarded as more of a request or question – is that the Review presents 
various analyses and discussion regarding firms but seems to have insufficient discussion regarding 
households. As mentioned, the Review points to the behavior and mindset based on the assumption that 
wages and prices will not increase easily as an important reason why prices remained stagnant. Indeed, 
firms – rather than households – might have prioritized maintaining employment over raising wages 
under the severe conditions in the labor market following the burst of the bubble economy. However, 
households' preference for low prices in an environment of stagnant wages might have helped form firms' 
behavior of keeping product prices unchanged. From this perspective, it would seem meaningful to 
deepen the analyses of households and prices by, for example, examining the characteristics of 
consumers' preference for lower prices and whether this has changed recently, the relationship between 
the economy and price preferences, or the more recent situation regarding inflation expectations 
formation in households by generation. 


 
195 
Review of Monetary Policy from a Broad Perspective, Bank of Japan 
Experts' Commentaries 
Professor SHIOJI Etsuro, Chuo University 
I welcome the Bank of Japan's initiative to conduct an official review of monetary policy from a broad 
perspective (hereafter, the Review). Before starting the main discussion of this commentary, I would like 
to touch on two points. First, Japan's financial markets during the period covered by the Review faced a 
series of significant shocks. In 1999, there was yet no prospect of Japan's nonperforming loan problem 
being resolved. The economy subsequently faced various shocks such as the Global Financial Crisis 
(GFC), the Great East Japan Earthquake, and the COVID-19 pandemic. Despite these multiple shocks, 
the economy did not fall into any serious financial panic. Even though this was not solely owing to the 
Bank's efforts, the Bank should be highly and fairly evaluated for its contributions, regardless of how one 
might feel about its monetary policy from a macroeconomic perspective. Second, a not insignificant 
portion of the costs of unconventional monetary policy measures will likely materialize during the process 
of normalizing the economy after the termination of the measures. The full extent of the financial loss to 
the Bank, for example, will become apparent only in the future. Therefore, it will take a few more years 
to reach a point at which we will be able to evaluate unconventional monetary policy in its entirety, based 
exclusively on observable data and facts. In other words, the "final exam" is still down the road. In this 
commentary, I present my assessment at this point in time, with reference to predictions for future 
developments. Specifically, I would like to discuss four topics that the Review did not explore in much 
depth. Then, I will briefly touch on a few other issues. 
1. On the Need to Clarify the Relation between Fiscal and Monetary Policies 
A foundation of conventional monetary policy is the separation of fiscal and monetary policies. There are 
two reasons why such a principle has been established. First, fiscal authorities have an incentive to rely 
on the seigniorage generated by monetary authorities. Therefore, a line between them needs to be drawn 
in order to remove such temptation. The second reason is that monetary authorities are not under the 
direct oversight of representatives who have been entrusted with authority by the public. Therefore, it is 
widely held that they should not wield any authority to conduct policy other than for the purpose of 
stabilizing inflation (and economic activities to some degree) through money market operations (another 
acceptable policy objective would be ensuring financial stability). In contrast, unconventional monetary 
policy starts out by intentionally removing this boundary, in order to cope with an extraordinary situation. 
It is therefore important to specify in advance the respective responsibilities of fiscal and monetary 
authorities, as well as limitations to the scope of their activities, during a period of unconventional 
monetary policy. In this regard, the joint statement issued by the government and the Bank in 2013 was 
groundbreaking. It states that the government and the Bank will work together toward overcoming 
deflation and that the government will establish a sustainable fiscal structure. However, this does not 
sufficiently clarify the nature of the cooperative relationship, and there remains room for improvement 
from the following four perspectives. (1) In the process of overcoming deflation, the Bank may be 
exposed to the risk of substantial financial losses. If the task of overcoming deflation is a joint effort by 
the government and the Bank, it would seem natural for the government to bear responsibility for 
compensating the Bank's losses. However, this obligation has not been explicitly spelled out. At the time 


 
          Review of Monetary Policy from a Broad Perspective, Bank of Japan 
Experts' Commentaries 
196 
of writing this commentary, a growing number of experts are expressing concern about the future of the 
Bank's standalone financial soundness, which suggests that this understanding – that the government 
bears responsibility for compensating losses – has not taken root in the private sector. The Review itself 
stresses the importance of ensuring the Bank's financial soundness. However, taking the spirit of the 
joint statement into account, the Bank should be assured of an environment in which it can focus on 
solidly establishing 2 percent inflation without worrying about the risks to its financial soundness. This 
should be clearly spelled out. In this regard, the Memorandum of Understanding on the Financial 
Relationship between HM Treasury and the Bank of England in the United Kingdom would be a good 
reference. (2) The Bank, under an unconventional policy framework, can access a wide range of policy 
measures. This enables it to undertake a certain kind of industrial policy – which may significantly impact 
resource allocation – and to influence the distribution of income and wealth to an extent unconceivable 
under a conventional policy framework. It should have been clarified in advance to what extent the Bank 
would be allowed to engage in such areas. Simply limiting the purpose of the Bank's policy to inflation 
and financial stability will not serve as a brake against the expansion of the scope of the Bank's 
responsibilities, as few economic phenomena are wholly unrelated to inflation and financial stability. If 
the Bank's responsibilities are expanded without limit, it will encounter greater pressure to use its great 
power for the benefit of all kinds of actors in society, which would also be undesirable for the Bank. (3) 
On a related note, under the conventional framework, there were various devices in place to make the 
boundary between the government and the Bank visible. For example, the so-called "banknote principle" 
– the limitation that the outstanding amount of long-term government bonds effectively held by the Bank 
should be kept below the outstanding balance of banknotes issued – was one of the ideas developed by 
our predecessors, albeit possibly not grounded in solid economic logic. Even if suspending this principle 
is temporarily unavoidable in response to an extraordinary environment, alternative ideas to limit the 
expansion of the Bank's responsibilities appear to be called for. A more in-depth discussion on this point 
should have been conducted in advance. (4) The issue of how the government would ensure its fiscal 
sustainability was not clarified in advance. As a result, concerns about the monetary financing of 
government debt have remained, as reflected in some of the comments from the private sector presented 
in the Review. 
2. Transmission of Monetary Policy Effects through Exchange Rates 
Needless to say, controlling exchange rates is not, nor should it be, a policy objective of the Bank. Still, 
exchange rates can be an important transmission channel of monetary easing, as the Review also 
acknowledges. Imported goods do not account for a significant portion of the production costs of 
consumer goods and services in Japan. However, the magnitude of exchange rate fluctuations itself is 
so large that they could have a non-trivial impact on domestic prices. In addition, exchange rates affect 
prices of many items that are regularly observed in people's daily lives, and thus also appear to have a 
significant impact on inflation expectations – an important variable for the Bank. It will be necessary in 
the future to closely re-examine how interest rate differentials between Japan and abroad play a role in 
determining exchange rates. Japan's inflation has finally exceeded 2 percent in the 2020s. This can be 


 
197 
Review of Monetary Policy from a Broad Perspective, Bank of Japan 
Experts' Commentaries 
attributed to the depreciation of the yen, which resulted from the widening of interest rate differentials 
between Japan and abroad, as central banks in the United States and Europe raised interest rates while 
the Bank of Japan patiently maintained its policy stance. Economists have often attributed the yen's 
appreciation during the period around the GFC to a quantitative aspect of monetary policies. That is, 
they point to the fact that the Bank of Japan did not follow central banks in the United States and Europe 
in initiating quantitative easing. However, it is unclear how important this effect was. Instead, a more 
important factor in the yen's appreciation seems to have been the narrowing of interest rate differentials, 
which occurred in an environment where Japan had little room to lower interest rates that were already 
close to zero while the United States and Europe still had some leeway. 
3. Quantity or Interest Rates? 
Related to the latter half of the previous section, the usefulness of quantity as a policy measure (e.g., the 
monetary base, the Bank’s holdings of Japanese government bonds (JGBs)) calls for further examination 
going forward. The Bank probably did not naively anticipate that expanding the monetary base would 
directly lead to an increase in money supply, in a situation in which nominal interest rates were stuck at 
the effective lower bound. If so, however, what would be an alternative policy transmission channel of 
the quantities? (For the discussion of the expectations channel, see the next section.) By comparison, 
transmission channels of interest rates have been better understood. In fact, the Bank shifted its main 
policy measure from quantity to interest rates (while still paying some attention to quantity) in fall 2016. I 
personally think that this move was appropriate, but further discussion will be called for going forward. It 
is true that, by 2016, the Bank had established an overwhelming presence in the JGB market, and that 
this presence was a precondition for it to effectively conduct Yield Curve Control (YCC). In that sense, it 
might be difficult to examine the impact of interest rate policy in complete isolation from quantity policy. 
However, consider, for example, the event in March 2020 during the early phase of the COVID-19 
pandemic, in which the Bank successfully suppressed sharp swings in long-term interest rates, in the 
face of sudden and significant fluctuations in the demand for JGBs. This helped stabilize the market 
quickly. This experience appears to be one indication of the superiority of YCC over policies that target 
quantities. 
4. "Communication" and "Commitment" 
The Bank has struggled to change private sector expectations, as the Review acknowledges. 
Emphasizing expectations formation itself is consistent with the trends in modern macroeconomics and 
thus is understandable. However, even after reading the Review, it is still hard to comprehend why the 
Bank expected the private sector to naively believe that the Bank would be able to achieve its intended 
policy goal, when the Bank apparently lacked effective policy measures to achieve it. The Review further 
emphasizes the adaptive, non-rational nature of the expectations of households and firms. However, is 
it not possible that the private sector just concluded, quite rationally, that the Bank lacked policy measures 
to achieve its declared target? Further examination of this topic is desirable going forward. Forward 
guidance through the bond market, where the Bank can exert a more direct control, seems to have been 


 
          Review of Monetary Policy from a Broad Perspective, Bank of Japan 
Experts' Commentaries 
198 
working effectively lately, but only since around the time of the termination of unconventional monetary 
policy in 2024, when market participants could clearly sense that the exit was coming in near future. 
Before that, Japan had been constrained by the zero lower bound on nominal interest rates for more 
than 10 years. Moreover, no end was clearly in sight, and it always felt like such a situation would persist 
for another 10 years or so. Under these circumstances, even when the Bank sent signals regarding 
policy action that it would take in distant future, my impression is that the market reactions were minimal. 
This contradicts the implications suggested by New Keynesian macroeconomic models. It seems that, 
in reality, we need to distinguish between "shallow zero interest rates" and "deep zero interest rates (or 
the zero interest rate swamp)". But what creates the difference between the two? I look forward to further 
analyses on this point as well. 
5. Other Points 
• One problem with unconventional policy is that, when it goes on for many years, it becomes difficult to 
know exactly where the true equilibrium of the financial markets is. Long-term interest rates in particular 
have remained at a low level, but it is unclear how much of the decline can be attributed to the effect of 
monetary policy in terms of reducing term premiums. This issue can affect judgements regarding the 
extent to which long-term interest rates will rise during the process of monetary policy normalization. 
Therefore, ongoing close examination is warranted. 
• In macroeconomics, the term "adaptive expectations formation," as cited above, means that private 
sector expectations gradually "trace" reality. However, I think that a better way to characterize the 
Japanese situation is that inflation expectations were firmly anchored, not at the policy target but at zero 
percent, barely responding even to actual price movements. Further discussion of this issue will also be 
necessary. 
• Regarding the current situation of Japan's economy, the Review offers the assessment that upward 
pressure on wages is intensifying due to labor shortages. I also believe this assessment is likely true, 
but at the same time cannot be fully confident of this. As the Review pointed out, room remains for women 
to extend working hours through measures such as transitioning to full-time employment. Looking at the 
developments of the labor force participation rate for senior men in the narrow age group of 65-69, this 
rate increased from 45.6 percent in 2004 to 63.8 percent in 2023. However, it was even higher in 1969 
at 68.3 percent. Although many workers were self-employed persons at that time and the industrial 
structure was also different, further analysis is needed to examine whether the upper bound of the labor 
force participation rate has in fact been reached. 
• The Bank has developed a number of new policy measures over its long history of unconventional 
policy. Examples include YCC, the three-tier system for current accounts at the Bank, and funds-
supplying operations. It is hoped that details of these policies (e.g., how financial markets were 
transformed under them) will be recorded. This would be a valuable asset not only for Japan but for many 
other economies in the world. 


 
 
199 
Review of Monetary Policy from a Broad Perspective, Bank of Japan 
Experts' Commentaries 
Professor TSURU Kotaro, Keio University 
The following is my commentary on the Bank's review of monetary policy from a broad perspective 
(hereinafter, the Review). First, I would like to commend the Bank for putting so much effort into 
incorporating a variety of insights and opinions in the course of formulating the Review, including hosting 
workshops to actively exchange opinions with experts and soliciting feedback from the general public. 
Additionally, requesting experts to freely offer their commentary before the Review's release, with the 
understanding that it would be published as is, was a bold and thoughtful decision by the Bank. With that 
in mind, I would like to discuss both the commendable aspects and some unaddressed issues of the 
Review. 
Two points can be highlighted as commendable aspects in the Review. The first is that it candidly 
acknowledges that some facets of monetary policy over the past 25 years require serious reflection. 
Considering that it is not easy for policy authorities, not just central banks, to objectively assess policies 
they have implemented in the past, I consider this a very bold decision. Specifically, first of all, the Review 
acknowledges that raising inflation expectations through influencing expectations was challenging, 
stating that "influencing expectations alone was not sufficiently effective to anchor inflation at 2 percent" 
(p.3), referring to "the difficulties of influencing expectations" (p.4) and stating that "the effect of the large-
scale monetary easing was not as large as expected at the time when it was introduced" (p.4). 
The Review also acknowledges that "amid the prolonged large-scale Japanese government bond (JGB) 
purchases, the functioning of the JGB market deteriorated as the effect of YCC" (p.4) and states that 
"there have been certain side effects on financial markets and financial institutions' profits" (p.5). It further 
states that "it may take a period of time for the functioning of the JGB market to recover" and that 
"possible side effects of large-scale monetary easing [may] materialize at a later date" (p.5). Particularly 
with respect to YCC, the idea of the central bank controlling long-term interest rates directly through the 
buying and selling of JGBs was considered taboo in monetary policy. Considering that the deterioration 
in market functioning and other side effects were to some extent predictable even before the measure 
was implemented, this issue was rightly recognized as problematic in the Review. 
The second point to be commended is that, in evaluating policy, the Review was committed to using a 
variety of models to clarify policy effects, both pros and cons, making use of empirical methods to the 
extent possible. The acknowledgment that "the effect [of large-scale monetary easing] was not as large 
as initially expected" (p.49) and that "the overall effect on the Japanese economy so far appears to have 
been positive" (p.5) represents reasonable assessments based on the results of the model analysis. It 
is not easy to measure the impact of large-scale monetary easing on the economy as a whole with a 
high degree of accuracy. However, as pointed out in the Review (p.4, p.50), the impact on economic 
activity and prices through developments in stock prices and exchange rates is important, and I believe 
that if there was a positive effect of large-scale monetary easing, those channels were the most 
substantial (Tsuru (2024b)). It should be emphasized that in Chart 1-3-16, stock price and exchange rate 
channels together account for 56 percent, or more than half, of the factors pushing up the output gap 


 
 
          Review of Monetary Policy from a Broad Perspective, Bank of Japan 
Experts' Commentaries 
200 
through the decline in interest rates. Conversely, it appears that the channel assumed by conventional 
monetary policy, through which growth in lending has a positive impact on economic activity and prices, 
was weak (Chart 1-3-15, the widening gap between the monetary base and M2 developments since 
2011). Furthermore, while it is fair that the analysis did not confirm the possibility that prolonged monetary 
easing had a positive long-term impact on the supply side of the economy (the so-called "high-pressure 
economy" argument), the hypothesis that a central bank could control the potential growth rate was fairly 
unreasonable to begin with. Given the stagnation in the accumulation of human capital by Japanese 
firms observed since the 2000s, it must be said that this hypothesis itself was not very realistic. In light 
of these issues, I appreciate the Review's frank recognition that, with regard to "the various 
unconventional monetary policy measures … the quantitative degree of their effects are uncertain ... 
unconventional monetary policy measures cannot fully substitute for conventional monetary policy 
measures" (p.6). As I mentioned above, my assessment is that the content of the Review is mostly 
appropriate. Still, there are some unaddressed issues to be raised. 
The first issue is the outlook for the "behavior and mindset based on the assumption that wages and 
prices will not increase easily" (hereinafter, the "behavior and mindset"), as well as the outlook for prices 
and wages. One reason the Review gives for the difficulty in changing such "behavior and mindset" is 
that "adaptive expectations formation has had a larger impact on the formation of inflation expectations 
in Japan, and such expectations also have been strongly influenced by past experience" (p.3). This 
conveys a sense that adaptive expectations formation in itself is problematic, but in reality, it is likely 
quite natural in terms of forming expectations. Instead, the Bank perhaps adhered too strongly to the 
conventional view that it can freely change economic agents' expectations as long as there is a 
commitment from policy authorities, grounded in the premise of rational expectations formation in 
macroeconomics. 
By using the phrase "behavior and mindset," the Review seems to assume something that will not change 
easily. At the same time, the Review states that "firms' behavior has shifted more toward raising wages 
and prices, which suggests a change in the "behavior and mindset" based on the assumption that wages 
and prices will not increase easily" (p.2) and that "the long-standing global disinflationary trend appears 
to be changing" (p.2). This suggests that the Bank sees the changes currently underway not as 
temporary but rather as permanent and structural, which strikes me as a somewhat optimistic outlook. 
Although the Review cites labor shortages as the basis for this view, it is important to note that labor 
shortages in 2019, prior to the pandemic, were on a par with the current level. I take the underlying notion 
here to be that the "behavior and mindset" in question are based only on a given "premise," and that if 
this premise changes – that is, if the situation in which wages and prices will not increase easily changes 
– then the "behavior and mindset" will also change accordingly. Even in such a case, however, the 
question is whether economic entities perceive the current situation of rising prices and wages as 
temporary or permanent and structural. The answer to that will determine the future direction of prices 
and wages, and ultimately of the "behavior and mindset." 
The Review considers this "behavior and mindset" within the framework of expectations formation in 


 
 
201 
Review of Monetary Policy from a Broad Perspective, Bank of Japan 
Experts' Commentaries 
conventional macroeconomics, but this needs to be revisited from the perspective of microeconomics 
and game theory. In particular, I believe a comparative institutional analysis approach would be useful 
here (see the reference material in Tsuru (2024c)). In other words, I consider this "behavior and mindset," 
like customs and social norms, to be what is referred to in comparative institutional analysis the 
equilibrium outcome of a game, that is, a stable behavioral pattern resulting from players acting 
strategically according to the rules of a game. My thought is that an equilibrium in which prices and 
wages will not rise has been chosen out of the combination of strategies on the part of various economic 
agents. In such a case, when this equilibrium comes about, it generates the shared expectation that 
prices and wages will not rise. This in turn reinforces the equilibrium, and the cycle repeats. To shift this 
equilibrium, it is necessary to confirm whether the rationality of the strategies chosen by the various 
economic agents is changing in response to changes in the environment. 
The reason why wages have not increased for a long time in this century is that both labor and 
management chose to maintain the employment of regular employees rather than raising base pay. 
Another element of the background is the fact that the existence of fixed wage hikes under membership-
based employment meant that full-time members of firms could enjoy a certain annual wage increase 
even if wages did not increase in the economy as a whole as firms left base pay unchanged. Furthermore, 
in terms of price setting, firms fell into a vicious cycle of cost- and price-cutting competition resulting from 
a failure of coordination (Bertrand competition of homogeneous goods), which led to a declining trend in 
price markups among firms and put downward pressure on wages (see Tsuru (2024a,c)). The proper 
interpretation in this context is that firms' price- and wage-setting behavior has changed in response to 
rising inflation, owing to strong exogenous shock factors; it is hard to imagine that the assumptions of 
the game played by economic agents have changed significantly. Asserting that the "behavior and 
mindset" have changed requires a recognition that the game assumptions have changed permanently. 
At present, however, there are no clear signs of structural changes in the labor markets or in the 
competitive environment and corporate behavior in the final goods markets that shaped the "behavior 
and mindset." 
The second issue is the price stability target of 2 percent. Even if we agree with the Review's claim that 
central banks "should aim for a positive inflation rate" (p.6), the question is whether it was appropriate 
for Japan to set a 2 percent target just because "many [central banks in major advanced economies] 
have set their inflation targets at 2 percent" (p.6). My impression is that the appropriateness of the price 
stability target of 2 percent has not been adequately analyzed. I bring up this issue because the economic 
conditions involving prices are quite different between Japan and other advanced economies. In addition 
to Japan's experience of long-term deflation, although an increase in prices brought about by external 
shocks after the outbreak of the pandemic was to some extent common across advanced economies, 
my impression is that the reaction of prices in Japan was slower and weaker. As Chart 1-3-4 shows, the 
strength of the adaptive expectations formation of inflation expectations also reflects Japan's unique 
context. The question remains whether the 2 percent target was too heavy a burden for Japan in the first 
place and whether it would have been better to set a target of around 1 percent. If the 2 percent target 


 
 
          Review of Monetary Policy from a Broad Perspective, Bank of Japan 
Experts' Commentaries 
202 
was deemed necessary to give the central bank adequate flexibility to set policy, one cannot be blamed 
for thinking that this is simply internal logic. Do people who are now struggling with the rising prices of 
food and other daily necessities really want prices to go up? Many survey respondents said they 
preferred a situation in which both prices and wages rise, but that does not imply that they think rising 
prices are good. The Review is correct to note that "it is necessary to achieve a moderate rise in prices 
accompanied by wage increases" (p.7) and, ultimately, it is fair to say that both people and the central 
bank think real wage increases are preferable. This can only be achieved through productivity growth, 
assuming that labor share and the terms of trade remain constant both for firms and for the country as a 
whole. However, productivity growth falls outside the scope of what monetary policy can control. In other 
words, if the potential growth rate and natural rate of interest decline, a reduction in monetary policy 
flexibility is inevitable. One can draw the conclusion that the widespread belief that the power of monetary 
policy and the central bank is unbounded was highly problematic. 
The third issue is the relationship with fiscal policy. In regards to whether "the large-scale monetary 
easing implemented by the Bank led to a loosening of fiscal discipline" (p.7), it is true that the Bank's 
monetary policy ended up playing, even if unintentionally, a complementary role in fiscal management, 
which has been chronically loose, including during the pandemic period when the government launched 
a fiscal stimulus package that was large in comparison with global standards. The Bank's commitment 
to purchasing an unlimited amount of JGBs, even from private financial institutions, indicates a situation 
that is extremely close to monetary finance. The Review states that "it is crucial for the Bank to continue 
to clearly explain … that the aim of monetary policy … should not be to support monetary financing" (p.7), 
but this can only be ensured by implementing policy autonomously, coupled with a commitment, rather 
than resorting to cheap talk.
