203 
Review of Monetary Policy from a Broad Perspective, Bank of Japan 
Experts' Commentaries 
Professor WATANABE Tsutomu, The University of Tokyo 
Comment 1: Is Chronic Deflation Caused by Aggregate Demand or Aggregate Supply? 
The review of monetary policy from a broad perspective (hereinafter, the Review) cites the low natural 
rate of interest and the resulting weakness in aggregate demand as causes of chronic deflation. The 
Bank lowered nominal interest rates and sought to raise inflation expectations to reduce real interest 
rates in line with the low natural rate of interest. However, real interest rates could not be lowered 
sufficiently to the level of the natural rate of interest, which led to shortages in aggregate demand – this 
is the scenario the Review sets out. 
If the low natural rate of interest and the resulting weakness in aggregate demand were the causes of 
chronic deflation, then there should have been an opposite development in the phase of overcoming 
chronic deflation starting in spring 2022. In reality, however, there is no evidence that the natural rate of 
interest has risen since spring 2022, nor has there been any evidence of growth in aggregate demand. 
Another scenario mentioned in the Review, though not the main one, involves entrenchment of the 
behavior and mindset that assume wages and prices will not rise easily (p.1). This is a distortion in pricing 
– price setting by firms and wage setting by labor unions – which means that the problem lay in aggregate 
supply, not aggregate demand. 
The "pricing distortion" scenario alone can account both for chronic deflation itself as well as for the 
process of overcoming deflation since spring 2022 (in other words, deflation was overcome because 
pricing by firms and labor unions returned to normal from spring 2022). Analysis needs to go one step 
further to explore whether the cause of chronic deflation lay in aggregate demand or in aggregate supply. 
If the cause of chronic deflation was aggregate supply rather than aggregate demand, this could alter 
the assessment of the Bank's policies. As Charts 1-1-13 and 1-1-14 show, pricing distortions by firms 
and labor unions began in the early 2000s at the latest. As far as I know, however, the Bank did not 
recognize such distortions at the time, and thus did not take any appropriate steps to correct them. It is 
possible that the behavior and mindset based on the assumption that wages and prices will not increase 
easily (p.1) became entrenched as a result of this long period of inaction. If this is true, then the Bank 
may have been deeply involved in the emergence of chronic deflation in the sense of pricing distortions. 
The Review lacks any analysis or consideration of the issue from this perspective. 
My understanding is that the Bank has recognized and attempted to correct pricing distortions (especially 
in terms of wage setting) since 2013, and is currently clarifying its stance toward the normalization of 
wage and price setting. This is commendable. 
Comment 2: What Were the Costs of Chronic Deflation? 
The most crucial points to consider when conducting a 25-year policy review are what harms chronic 
deflation caused and to what extent the Bank's policies have eliminated those harms. Although the 
Review does admit that "in an environment with wage rigidity, it can be difficult to adjust wages according 


 
          Review of Monetary Policy from a Broad Perspective, Bank of Japan 
Experts' Commentaries 
204 
to the productivity levels of individual workers or firms" (Appendix 15, p.175), there is virtually no 
discussion of how the Bank perceived the costs of chronic deflation or whether its policies could have 
mitigated such costs. 
To give an illustration, if a person is debating whether or not to take a medication, two things need to be 
quantified and compared: (1) what part of the body will worsen if the medicine is not taken and to what 
extent, and (2) how much will the medicine improve the person's physical condition and what side effects 
will it have. To my knowledge, however, the Bank has never sent out a clear message regarding the first 
question. Even in the latest Review, there is much talk about the second question but no discussion 
about the first. 
Needless to say, we cannot come to a conclusion by ignoring the negative consequences of not taking 
an action and discussing only the effects and side effects of taking the action. I think this is part of the 
reason why discussion of the Bank's policies over the past 25 years has been confused. Trying to spell 
out the costs of chronic deflation is a difficult question, but that does not warrant simply leaving the 
question aside. I look forward to future analysis. 
Comment 3: Counterfactual Simulations 
Charts 1-3-12 and 1-3-14 show counterfactual simulations carried out using a factor-augmented vector 
autoregressive (FAVAR) model and other methods, which offer useful information contributing to the 
quantitative assessment of the effects of the Bank's policies. These simulations aimed to find out what 
would have happened if the Bank had not implemented Quantitative and Qualitative Monetary Easing 
(QQE) since 2013. 
This is useful, of course, but what I consider more important is the counterfactual question of what would 
have happened if the Bank had introduced inflation targeting and implemented QQE before 2013 (for 
example, in the early 2000s). Had the Bank adopted such measures in the early 2000s, this might have 
been expected to have the effect of pushing up employment, wages, and prices, and avoided 
entrenchment of the behavior and mindset based on the assumption that wages and prices will not 
increase easily (p.1). 
The main focus of the Review was to assess the consequences (the effects and side effects) of what the 
Bank did. To properly evaluate policy conduct, however, I think it is equally important or even more so to 
explore the consequences of what the Bank did not do, even though it should have. 
Comment 4: Zero Lower Bound on Nominal Interest Rates 
The reason why the Bank's policies over the past 25 years are considered "unconventional" is because 
Japan has reached the zero lower bound on nominal interest rates since 1999. All of the Bank's measures 
over this period were attempts to break through this zero lower bound. The Review sums up these 
attempts by concluding that "unconventional monetary policy measures cannot fully substitute for 
conventional monetary policy measures that control short-term interest rates" (p.6). 


 
205 
Review of Monetary Policy from a Broad Perspective, Bank of Japan 
Experts' Commentaries 
What implications can be drawn from the lesson that unconventional measures cannot fully substitute 
for conventional measures? First, the essential thing is to obviate the need to resort to the use of 
unconventional measures – in other words, to avoid reaching the zero lower bound on nominal interest 
rates. To this end, it is necessary to set higher interest rates during normal times. The Review says that 
setting the inflation target at 2 percent (rather than at zero) would contribute to this (p.6). 
Although further analysis is needed as to whether the 2 percent level is enough to secure sufficient 
leeway for lowering interest rates, this is a reasonable direction going forward. The series of interest rate 
hikes the Bank has implemented since March 2024 can also be assessed as useful in terms of avoiding 
reaching the zero lower bound on interest rates (thus avoiding the need to resort again to unconventional 
measures). 
The second implication is that the Bank should explore removing the zero lower bound on nominal 
interest rates itself. The Negative Interest Rate Policy introduced in 2016 was one such attempt, but 
negative interest rates were only applied to current accounts at the Bank, and not to banknotes. This 
gave rise to concerns about the problem of the "reversal rate" (p.57). Removing the zero lower bound 
on nominal interest rates entirely necessitates applying negative interest rates to all money, including 
banknotes. 
Complete removal of the zero lower bound on nominal interest rates is often considered unrealistic. In 
fact, the Review does not mention the possibility at all. However, if the conclusion, after the Bank for 25 
years has attempted various unconventional measures in the face of the zero lower bound on nominal 
interest rates, is that the zero lower bound cannot be overcome, then the next step to consider should 
be removing the bound altogether. 
Given the low natural rate of interest in Japan, unfortunately, the probability of reaching the zero lower 
bound on nominal interest rates again is far from inconceivable, and in fact should be viewed as fairly 
high. The Bank should start now laying the groundwork for removing this constraint when that day comes. 
Comment 5: Causality of Leaving Prices and Wages Unchanged 
The Review evinces a recognition that, over the past 25 years, the phenomena of leaving prices and 
wages unchanged have both occurred simultaneously. At the same time, it claims the causal relationship 
between the two is one in which leaving prices unchanged leads to leaving wages unchanged. Appendix 
4, for example, states that "Japanese firms secured their earnings by continuing to suppress cost pass-
through with declining price markups, while strengthening the tendency to suppress wages with 
increased wage markdowns" (p.103). Numerous statements elsewhere in the Review assume that 
leaving prices unchanged leads to leaving wages unchanged. 
As Chart 1-1-23 shows, however, the decline in price markups and the increase in wage markdowns 
have occurred virtually simultaneously. It is not clear from this chart which is the cause and which is the 
effect, and the Review makes no attempt to analyze the direction of the causality it assumes. The 
possibility has not been ruled out that the causality is the opposite of what the Review assumes – that 


 
          Review of Monetary Policy from a Broad Perspective, Bank of Japan 
Experts' Commentaries 
206 
leaving wages unchanged leads to leaving prices unchanged. In other words, freezes on base pay raises 
among other factors have led to the increase in wage markdowns, which in turn made it possible to 
reduce price markups. 
In explaining price and wage movements during this period and considering the appropriateness of the 
Bank's policies, it is vital to determine whether the driving force was prices or wages. I look forward to 
further analysis on this score. 
 


 
207 
Review of Monetary Policy from a Broad Perspective, Bank of Japan 
Experts' Commentaries 
Professor Emeritus YOSHIKAWA Hiroshi, The University of Tokyo 
The Bank of Japan's review of monetary policy from a broad perspective (hereinafter, the Review) covers 
the past 25 years or so in Japan since the burst of the bubble economy in the early 1990s. However, the 
most relevant topic in terms of both public interest and the Bank's ongoing steps to normalize policy is 
the Review's assessment of the so-called Quantitative and Qualitative Monetary Easing (QQE) the Bank 
put in place over the 10 years from April 2013 to March 2023 under the direction of Governor Haruhiko 
Kuroda. I would thus like to focus my comments on monetary policy over the past 10 years. 
Chapter I of the Review divides the 25-year period into monetary policy conduct until the early 2010s 
followed by the impact of large-scale monetary easing since 2013 on economic activity and prices. 
Discussion of the latter period begins by noting that "the Bank set a price stability target of 2 percent in 
January 2013 and introduced QQE in April of that year." However, the Accord between the Bank and the 
government, concluded in January 2013 under the previous Governor Masaaki Shirakawa, was based 
on an entirely different basic approach to monetary policy than the approach the Bank began taking in 
April under Governor Kuroda. I find it hard to accept the Review's matter-of-fact discussion here, which 
implies that there was no gap between the two approaches. The proper dividing line is not "since 2013" 
but "since April 2013," as shown in Chart 3. 
The QQE that began in April 2013 was an unprecedented monetary policy measure based on a clear 
rationale, involving three claims. 
(1) The main cause of long-term stagnation that began in Japan in the 1990s was deflation, a situation 
that no other advanced economy had experienced since the end of World War II. 
(2) Since deflation, like inflation, is a monetary phenomenon, it can be stopped by sufficiently increasing 
the volume of money. 
(3) If nominal interest rates fall to a lower bound, such as zero, a sufficient increase in the money supply 
(money stock) can raise inflation expectations among economic agents, reduce real interest rates, 
and help recovery in the economy (alongside a rise in actual prices). 
This understanding and the consequent rationale for monetary policy have been advocated since the 
1990s by many American macroeconomists, as well as Japanese economists who share similar views. 
Krugman (1998) was the pioneer of the model supporting point (3) above. However, I believe all three 
claims are incorrect (Yoshikawa (2013)). 
Regarding deflation, a distinction needs to be made between the kind of severe deflation experienced 
during the Great Depression of the 1930s and the kind of mild deflation Japan experienced from the late 
1990s. As Fisher and Keynes pointed out, the kind of severe deflation the world experienced in the 1930s 
is the greatest threat to capitalist economies. However, it cannot generally be held that mild deflation 
presents the greatest threat to the macroeconomy – as evidenced not least by the historical fact that, in 
the mid-19th century, the British economy during the reign of Queen Victoria was at the forefront of the 


 
          Review of Monetary Policy from a Broad Perspective, Bank of Japan 
Experts' Commentaries 
208 
world, enjoying high real economic growth even while experiencing mild deflation over several decades. 
In fact, this has been confirmed in many postwar economies (Borio et al. (2015)). It is this kind of mild 
deflation that Japan experienced starting in the late 1990s. 
What is directly relevant to the Review is the assessment of the claims enumerated in points (2) and (3) 
above – namely, the unprecedented monetary easing policy known as reflation. The second Abe Shinzo 
Cabinet, formed out of the December 2012 general election, set out an economic policy, termed 
"Abenomics," which consisted of "three arrows." The "first arrow" was a bold monetary easing policy. At 
a policy meeting in April 2013, immediately after assuming his position, the newly appointed Governor 
Kuroda pledged that the Bank would double the monetary base in two years, thereby achieving the target 
of a 2 percent increase in consumer prices in that timeframe. 
The pledge to double the monetary base in two years was kept, but the 2 percent inflation target was not 
reached. The Bank subsequently introduced negative interest rates in January 2016 and 10-year JGB 
yield curve control in September of the same year. Eventually, the unprecedented easing accompanied 
by large-scale JGB purchases continued until March 2024. 
Regarding the positive effects of such large-scale monetary easing, the Review acknowledges that (1) 
the measures did not have as large an upward effect on prices as originally expected, partly because it 
was not easy to influence expectations, but says that (2) they did have a positive effect to some degree 
on economic activity and prices. The Review mentions that easing had side effects, including a significant 
decline in the functioning of the Japanese government bond (JGB) market, but concludes that, "although 
there have been certain side effects on financial markets and financial institutions' profits, the overall 
effect on the Japanese economy so far appears to have been positive." 
I cannot agree with this conclusion. 
At six and a half pages, the main text of the Review itself is concise, but it includes 15 appendixes and 
numerous charts. My sense, however, is that it is seeking to look in detail at various trees while not 
attempting to see the forest. As the figures make clear, the fact cannot be ignored that large-scale 
monetary easing failed to achieve the 2 percent inflation target. Naturally, the biggest question is why. 
The Review's argument is as follows: "…large-scale monetary easing appears to have had an impact to 
some degree on inflation expectations (forward-looking expectations formation). However, adaptive 
expectations formation has had a larger impact on the formation of inflation expectations in Japan, and 
such expectations also have been strongly influenced by past experience. It was not easy to change the 
behavior and mindset based on the assumption that wages and prices will not increase easily, and 
influencing expectations alone was not sufficiently effective to anchor inflation at 2 percent." As 
enumerated in points (2) and (3) above about the rationale behind QQE, this kind of monetary policy is 
simply the basic framework of the reflationary school of economics, which has been advocated by many 
American economists since Krugman and is said to be in line with global standards. 
According to the Review's summary, the reflationary policy of influencing expectations was correct but 


 
209 
Review of Monetary Policy from a Broad Perspective, Bank of Japan 
Experts' Commentaries 
the objectives were not achieved because of the poor constitution of Japan's economy. My view, however, 
is that the objectives were not achieved because the theoretical framework of such a reflationary policy 
itself was fundamentally flawed (Yoshikawa (2022)). 
It goes without saying that when talking about prices it is necessary to know how they are determined. It 
is no exaggeration to say that asset prices such as stock prices and exchange rates, as well as the prices 
of primary commodities such as oil and wheat, are determined by expectations. However, many 
economists since Kalecki (1939) have held that the prices of goods and services that make up the 
consumer price index (CPI) are entirely different from the prices of primary commodities, and that goods 
and services prices are determined by individual suppliers (firms) based on the notion of "normal cost." 
Expectations have no role to play in this. 
On this point, I would like to comment on the theory of the kinked demand curve mentioned in Appendix 
4, as there appears to be some confusion among economists today. For example, Watanabe (2024) 
writes that the kinked demand curve is a theory explaining why prices are kept constant (p.49), but this 
statement is inaccurate. When the individual demand curves of firms under imperfect competition are 
kinked, one firm is conscious of the actions of its rivals and is cautious about going it alone in raising 
prices. Negishi (1979) showed that, in this framework, growth in real demand is absorbed by changes in 
quantity and prices are kept unchanged (a microfoundation of Keynes's principle of effective demand, in 
which "quantity" determines "quantity"). This does not prevent firms from raising prices, however. Firms 
facing a kinked demand curve will be cautious about raising prices, but this is only in terms of the actions 
of single firms. When all firms in a single industry or sector raise prices, uniform price increases occur. 
What makes firms in an industry supplying similar goods or services feel the need to raise prices 
uniformly is rising costs. It follows that the kinked demand curve is actually a theory that explains normal 
cost pricing (Ito (1965)). In fact, inflation in Japan since 2022 began with the rise in the international 
prices of primary commodities and import prices, which moved from upstream to downstream, affecting 
numerous industries uniformly. 
The Review is also mistaken in describing adaptive expectations formation as if it were the opposite of 
forward-looking expectations formation, such as the formation of rational expectations (Muth (1960)). 
This is something I have pointed out many times, and it is regrettable that the Review repeats this same 
mistake. Expectations are adaptive because consumers understand that real prices, unlike asset prices, 
have strong inertia (Ueda and Yoshikawa (1986)). 
What kind of impact did large-scale easing have on the real economy? The Review uses the phrase 
"economic activity and prices." Economic activity likely refers to the real economy, such as real GDP. It 
states that "large-scale monetary easing likely worked to push up economic activity and prices." It refers 
to analyses using several different models as the basis for this conclusion. However, it is easy to show 
"some extent" of effect in an econometric model. Developments in Japan's economy over the past 10 
years of large-scale easing do not track with the Review's conclusion, though. The average annual 
growth rate of real private consumption from 2013 to 2019 was 0.0 percent (the rate was negative if data 


 
          Review of Monetary Policy from a Broad Perspective, Bank of Japan 
Experts' Commentaries 
210 
following the onset of the pandemic in 2020 are included), so it cannot be said that monetary easing 
helped boost consumption (the period saw an ongoing decline in the propensity to consume). It is a well-
known fact that firms' business fixed investment and research and development investment remained 
lackluster. Logically, an argument could be made that both consumption and investment would have 
fallen even further without large-scale easing, but there is insufficient evidence to show this. 
Finally, the Review cautiously states that it will be important to weigh the benefits and costs of 
unconventional monetary policy measures, but it also says that the overall effect on the Japanese 
economy appears to have been positive. If so, the logical extension of this argument suggests that even 
mild deflation would justify repeating such policy measures. But is that acceptable?

229 
Review of Monetary Policy from a Broad Perspective, Bank of Japan 
Summary of Changes in Monetary Policy Management 
Summary of Changes in Monetary Policy Management 
Feb. 1999 
Introduction of the Zero Interest Rate Policy 
 
The Bank of Japan will provide more ample funds and encourage the uncollateralized 
overnight call rate to move as low as possible. 
Aug. 2000 
Termination of the Zero Interest Rate Policy 
 
The Bank of Japan will encourage the uncollateralized overnight call rate to move on 
average around 0.25%. 
Oct. 2000 
Release of "On Price Stability" 
Feb. 2001 
Change in the Guideline of Money Market Operations 
 
Encourage the uncollateralized overnight call rate to move on average around 0.15 
percent. 
The Establishment of Complementary Lending Facility 
Mar. 2001 
Introduction of the Quantitative Easing Policy 
 
The Bank of Japan will conduct money market operations, aiming the outstanding 
balance of the current accounts at the Bank at around 5 trillion yen. Should there be 
a risk of financial market instability, e.g., a rapid surge in liquidity demand, the Bank 
will provide ampler liquidity irrespective of the guideline above. 
 
Strong Commitment in terms of Policy Duration 
June 2003 
Introduction of Purchases of Asset-Backed Securities 
Apr. 2004 
Introduction of the Securities Lending Facility to Provide the Markets with a Secondary 
Source of Japanese Government Securities 
Mar. 2006 
Termination of the Quantitative Easing Policy 
 
The Bank of Japan will encourage the uncollateralized overnight call rate to remain at 
effectively zero percent. 
Release of "The Bank's Thinking on Price Stability" 
 
An approximate range between zero and two percent of year-on-year change in the 
consumer price index was generally consistent with the distribution of each Board 
member's understanding of medium- to long-term price stability (median figures fell 
on both sides of one percent). 
July 2006 
Change in the Guideline of Money Market Operations 
 
The Bank of Japan will encourage the uncollateralized overnight call rate to remain at 
around 0.25 percent. 
Feb. 2007 
Change in the Guideline of Money Market Operations 
 
The Bank of Japan will encourage the uncollateralized overnight call rate to  
remain at around 0.5 percent. 
Sept. 2008 
The Bank of Japan, the European Central Bank, the Bank of England, the Bank of Canada, 
and the Swiss National Bank have established the temporary U.S. dollar swap agreements 


 
 
         Review of Monetary Policy from a Broad Perspective, Bank of Japan 
Summary of Changes in Monetary Policy Management 
230 
with the Federal Reserve respectively. The Bank of Japan has introduced the scheme 
supplying U.S. dollars within the value of collateral on a fixed interest rate basis (U.S. Dollar

Dec. 2009 
Release of "Clarification of the Understanding of Medium- to Long-Term Price Stability" 
 
The Policy Board does not tolerate a year-on-year rate of change in the CPI equal to 
or below 0 percent. The midpoints of most Policy Board members' "understanding" 
are around 1 percent. 
May 2010 
The Bank of Japan, the European Central Bank, the Bank of England, the Bank of Canada, 
and the Swiss National Bank have re-established the temporary U.S. dollar swap 
agreements with the Federal Reserve respectively. The Bank of Japan has restarted the 
U.S. Dollar Funds-Supplying Operations against Pooled Collateral. 
June 2010 
Introduction of the Fund-Provisioning Measure to Support Strengthening the Foundations 
for Economic Growth 
 
The fund-provisioning measure supplies long-term funds at a low interest rate to 
financial institutions in accordance with their efforts in terms of lending and investment 
toward strengthening the foundations for economic growth. 
Oct. 2010 
Introduction of Comprehensive Monetary Easing 
 
The Bank of Japan will encourage the uncollateralized overnight call rate to remain at 
around 0 to 0.1 percent. 
 
Clarification of policy time horizon 
 
Establishment of an Asset Purchase Program 
・ The Bank will examine establishing, as a temporary measure, a program on its 
balance sheet to purchase various financial assets, such as government securities, 
commercial paper (CP), corporate bonds, exchange-traded funds (ETFs), and 
Japan real estate investment trusts (J-REITs) and to conduct the fixed-rate funds-
supplying operation against the pooled collateral.

231 
Review of Monetary Policy from a Broad Perspective, Bank of Japan 
Summary of Changes in Monetary Policy Management 
Nov. 2011 
The Bank of Japan, the Federal Reserve, the European Central Bank, the Bank of 
England, the Bank of Canada, and the Swiss National Bank have agreed to establish 
temporary bilateral liquidity swap arrangements so that liquidity can be provided in each 
jurisdiction in any of their currencies.  
Feb. 2012 
Release of "The Price Stability Goal in the Medium to Long Term" 
 
The Bank judges that "the price stability goal in the medium to long term" is in a 
positive range of 2 percent or lower in terms of the year-on-year rate of change in the 
consumer price index (CPI) and, more specifically, set a goal at 1 percent for the time 
being.  
Oct. 2012 
Release of "Measures Aimed at Overcoming Deflation" 
Dec. 2012 
Introduction of the Stimulating Bank Lending Facility 
 
The Stimulating Bank Lending Facility aims to provide long-term funds -- up to the 
amount equivalent to the net increase in lending -- at a low interest rate, without any 
limit, to financial institutions at their request, with a view to promoting their aggressive 
action and helping increase proactive credit demand of firms and households. 
Jan. 2013 
Introduction of the "Price Stability Target" of 2 percent 
 
The Bank sets the "price stability target" at 2 percent in terms of the year-on-year rate 
of change in the consumer price index (CPI) -- a main price index and will pursue 
monetary easing and aim to achieve this target at the earliest possible time. 
Introduction of the "Open-Ended Asset Purchasing Method" 
Release of "Joint Statement of the Government and the Bank of Japan on Overcoming 
Deflation and Achieving Sustainable Economic Growth" 
Apr. 2013 
Introduction of the "Quantitative and Qualitative Monetary Easing" 
 
The adoption of the "monetary base control" 
 
An increase in JGB purchases and their maturity extension 
 
An increase in ETF and J-REIT purchases 
 
The Bank will continue with the quantitative and qualitative monetary easing, aiming 
to achieve the price stability target of 2 percent, as long as it is necessary for 
maintaining that target in a stable manner.

Jan. 2016 
Introduction of "Quantitative and Qualitative Monetary Easing with a Negative Interest 
Rate" 
 
The Bank will apply a negative interest rate of minus 0.1 percent to current accounts 
that financial institutions hold at the Bank. Specifically, the Bank will adopt a three-tier 
system in which the outstanding balance of each financial institution's current account 
at the Bank will be divided into three tiers (Basic Balance, Macro Add-on Balance, and 
Policy-Rate Balance), to each of which a positive interest rate, a zero interest rate, or 
a negative interest rate will be applied, respectively. 
 
The Bank will continue with "QQE with a Negative Interest Rate," aiming to achieve 
the price stability target of 2 percent, as long as it is necessary for maintaining that 
target in a stable manner. 
Apr. 2016 
Introduction of the Funds-Supplying Operation to Support Financial Institutions in Disaster 
Areas of the 2016 Kumamoto Earthquake 
July 2016 
Enhancement of Monetary Easing 
 
An increase in purchases of exchange-traded funds (ETFs) 
 
Measures to ensure smooth funding in foreign currencies by Japanese firms and 
financial institutions 
Sept. 2016 
Introduction of "Quantitative and Qualitative Monetary Easing with Yield Curve Control" 
 
Yield curve control 
・ The short-term policy interest rate:  
The Bank will apply a negative interest rate of minus 0.1 percent to the Policy-Rate 
Balances in current accounts held by financial institutions at the Bank.  
・ The long-term interest rate:  
The Bank will purchase Japanese government bonds (JGBs) so that 10-year JGB 
yields will remain more or less at the current level (around zero percent). With regard 
to the amount of JGBs to be purchased, the Bank will conduct purchases more or 
less in line with the current pace -- an annual pace of increase in the amount 
outstanding of its JGB holdings at about 80 trillion yen -- aiming to achieve the target 
level of a long-term interest rate specified by the guideline. 
 
New tools of market operations for facilitating yield curve control 
・ Outright purchases of JGBs with yields designated by the Bank (fixed-rate purchase 
operations) 
・ Fixed-rate funds-supplying operations for a period of up to 10 years (extending the 
longest maturity of the operation from 1 year at present) 
 
Inflation-overshooting commitment 
・ The Bank will continue with "QQE with Yield Curve Control," aiming to achieve the 
price stability target of 2 percent, as long as it is necessary for maintaining that 
target in a stable manner. The Bank will continue expanding the monetary base 
until the year-on-year rate of increase in the observed CPI (all items less fresh food)

exceeds the price stability target of 2 percent and stays above the target in a stable

July 2018 
Strengthening the Framework for Continuous Powerful Monetary Easing 
 
Introduction of forward guidance for policy rates 
・ The Bank intends to maintain the current extremely low levels of short- and long-
term interest rates for an extended period of time. 
 
Enhancement of the sustainability of "Quantitative and Qualitative Monetary Easing 
(QQE) with Yield Curve Control" 
・ The Bank will purchase Japanese government bonds (JGBs) so that 10-year JGB 
yields will remain at around zero percent. While doing so, the yields may move 
upward and downward to some extent mainly depending on developments in 
economic activity and prices. With regard to the amount of JGBs to be purchased, 
the Bank will conduct purchases in a flexible manner so that their amount 
outstanding will increase at an annual pace of about 80 trillion yen. 
・ The Bank will purchase exchange-traded funds (ETFs) and Japan real estate 
investment trusts (J-REITs) so that their amounts outstanding will increase at 
annual paces of about 6 trillion yen and about 90 billion yen, respectively. With a 
view to lowering risk premia of asset prices in an appropriate manner, the Bank may 
increase or decrease the amount of purchases depending on market conditions.

Sept. 2021 
Introduction of the Funds-Supplying Operations to Support Financing for Climate Change 
Responses 
Dec. 2022 
Modification of the Conduct of Yield Curve Control (YCC) 
 
While significantly increasing the amount of JGB purchases, the Bank will expand the 
range of 10-year JGB yield fluctuations from the target level: from between around 
plus and minus 0.25 percentage points to between around plus and minus 0.5 
percentage points. 
Jan. 2023 
Enhancement of the Funds-Supplying Operations against Pooled Collateral 
July 2023 
Conducting Yield Curve Control (YCC) with Greater Flexibility 
 
The Bank will continue to allow 10-year JGB yields to fluctuate in the range of around 
plus and minus 0.5 percentage points from the target level, while it will conduct yield 
curve control with greater flexibility, regarding the upper and lower bounds of the range 
as references, not as rigid limits, in its market operations. 
Oct. 2023 
Further Increasing the Flexibility in the Conduct of Yield Curve Control (YCC) 
 
The Bank will regard the upper bound of 1.0 percent for 10-year JGB yields as a 
reference in its market operations, and in order to encourage the formation of a yield 
curve that is consistent with the guideline for market operations, it will continue with 
large-scale JGB purchases and make nimble responses for each maturity by, for 
example, increasing the amount of JGB purchases and conducting fixed-rate 
purchase operations and the Funds-Supplying Operations against Pooled Collateral. 
Mar. 2024 
Changes in the Monetary Policy Framework 
 
The Bank will encourage the uncollateralized overnight call rate to remain at around 
0 to 0.1 percent. 
 
Purchase of Japanese government bonds (JGBs) 
・ The Bank will continue its JGB purchases with broadly the same amount as before. 
 
The Bank will discontinue purchases of exchange-traded funds (ETFs) and Japan real 
estate investment trusts (J-REITs). 
 
The Bank will gradually reduce the amount of purchases of CP and corporate bonds 
and will discontinue the purchases in about one year. 
July 2024 
Change in the Guideline for Money Market Operations 
 
The Bank will encourage the uncollateralized overnight call rate to remain at around 
0.25 percent. 
Decision on the Plan for the Reduction of the Purchase Amount of Japanese Government 
Bonds
